At each tested cell, semivariance was residualized against age, log ET volume, shape anisotropy, raw T1c mean and standard deviation, and sex. The primary additional texture adjustment added five principal components of the 468 original oriented GLCM features, with ten components examined as sensitivity. Principal component analysis was applied to the GLCM control data only; native MUSE was neither decomposed nor replaced by a fitted field. Cells entered testing according to label-blind coverage; patient-level labels were permuted 2,000 times within strata defined by age, tumor volume, and sex. Standardized signed cell scores contributed to a global mean-squared-score statistic. The maximum absolute permuted cell score provided family-wise error rate (FWER) adjustment over all 2,508 tested cells. This is an exploratory conditional permutation design; exchangeability within the strata and representativeness of the partial GLCM adjustment are assumptions, not guaranteed properties.

A previously completed secondary comparison of compact fitted MUSE summaries and radiomics was retained solely for context, including internally validated IDH and survival models. It is reported separately from the original-field experiment. It cannot establish or refute incremental clinical utility of the complete 328-curve field.

Outcome-independent illustrative case selection.

A set of 15 opposite-IDH grade-4 case pairs was selected using radiomic proximity and conventional demographic/morphological constraints without screening native MUSE curves for favorable differences. The original export contained 83,875 semivariance observations spanning 9,512 complete experimental curves from 29 unique tumors, together with 468 oriented GLCM measures per tumor and their clinical metadata. The 15 pairs served as illustrative and negative-control candidates; matched IDH labels by construction cannot establish diagnostic association. Selected examples were displayed with the full original curves and original GLCM-offset values. When scale-band differences were tabulated, these were explicitly secondary descriptions of the displayed native measurements.

3.5 Independent convolutional classification benchmark

An independent 3D residual convolutional neural network was evaluated for patient-level classification of IDH-mutant versus IDH-wildtype gliomas. The architecture was an 18-layer residual design adapted to volumetric convolutions, trained from random initialization [36]. The two input channels were a robustly normalized T1c volume and the binary ET mask. Intensity normalization used the median and interquartile range of nonzero voxels within each T1c volume, followed by clipping to [-6, 6]. The volume and mask were cropped or zero-padded to a 96 × 96 × 96 voxel cube centered at the ET centroid. No interpolation of MRI voxels was performed during cropping; the CNN input therefore retained local anatomy and the segmentation geometry. Random flips along the three volume axes and small Gaussian perturbations of the normalized intensity channel were applied only during training.

The benchmark used the 416 independent baseline patients with MUSE eligibility and known IDH status (43 mutant and 373 wildtype), preserving the five preassigned, patient-disjoint, stratified outer folds. Each training partition was separated once into an internal model-fitting subset and a stratified validation subset containing 15% of that partition. Optimization used AdamW (learning rate 10 -4 , weight decay 10 -4 ), weighted binary cross-entropy with the training-set ratio of negative to positive cases, batch size 2, and up to 100 epochs. Validation ROC-AUC controlled early stopping after 15 epochs without improvement; the best validation checkpoint was evaluated once on each held-out outer fold. No pretrained weights or external training cohort were employed. The pooled out-of-fold predictions produced the reported classification metrics; sensitivity, specificity, and balanced accuracy used a fixed decision threshold of 0.5. The CNN's sigmoid scores were not clinically calibrated probabilities.

The independent CNN analysis supplied an image-based prediction for each eligible patient. For direct descriptive correspondence, we also assessed its already computed out-of-fold scores on the subset of 375 subjects shared with the native MUSE and oriented GLCM study. This restriction changes the evaluation population but does not retrain the CNN or alter its original outer-fold predictions. Native MUSE curves and GLCM observations were inspected after classification in selected cases to contextualize spatial organization. The CNN and variographic field remained independent computational outputs; no radiomic or MUSE measurements entered the network architecture. Clinical interpretation distinguishes molecular IDH status from benign versus malignant diagnosis or WHO grade.

4. Experimental Results

The experimental evaluation examines how original MUSE direction-dependent semivariance measurements relate to multiscale radiomic statistics and to the molecular annotations of the same glioma cohort. The analysis first verifies the source archive and physical correspondence between representations, then evaluates between-patient agreement and voxel-pair support. The subsequent experiments investigate IDH associations across native direction and lag coordinates, illustrate representative spatial patterns, and report independent CNN classification with case-level post-prediction characterization.

4.1 Technical feasibility and complete-field integrity

The original MUSE archive contained 136,448 experimental directional curves and 1,151,687 original directional lag observations across 416 baseline cases. The subset of 375 patients with directional GLCMs contained 123,000 curves and 1,075,217 lag observations. Every native measurement could be traced to the frozen subject source JSON using source-hash verification. Four ET-positive acquisitions had technical MUSE failures, and examinations with no enhancing voxels could not yield an ET-restricted representation. No post hoc removal was applied solely because an observed variogram was unusually anisotropic. The individual 328-curve fields are the primary result of MUSE , and all tabulated scalar statistics in this section quantify aspects of their comparison without replacing the fields.

In the 416-subject baseline cohort, the available conventional analysis included 396 grade-4 tumors; 43 cases were IDH-mutant and 373 were IDH-wildtype. The paired 375-subject cohort comprised 33 IDH-mutant and 342 wildtype tumors. As the ROI was defined by enhancement, the analyzed cohort was strongly enriched for enhancing grade-4 gliomas. The ET-absent exclusion is therefore also a biological selection criterion, not merely an acquisition failure.

4.2 Geometric compatibility between native MUSE and GLCM

The 375-patient paired dataset yielded 29,250 requested GLCM offset observations (78 per case), of which 28,683 met the GLCM support gate. Under the primary angle-lag-MUSE -support thresholds, 9,371 offsets (32.7% of supported GLCM offsets) had at least one geometrically matching MUSE measurement; under the strict criteria, 2,254 (7.9%) were matched. All 78 GLCM orientations lay within about 2.8 • of some MUSE direction, indicating that physical-lag compatibility was a primary determinant of missing matches. Across the selected matches, only 4,581 unique native MUSE curves were involved, approximately 3.7% of all available paired-cohort curves. Unmatched curves were retained and cannot be equated with new or exclusive biological information.

Geometric compatibility diminished as the physical length of the tested offset increased. Of supported step-1 offsets, 4,116 of 4,864 (84.6%) met the primary match definition. The corresponding figures were 2,618/4,861 (53.9%) for step 2, 1,122/4,852 (23.1%) for step 3, 1,129/4,809 (23.5%) for step 5, 35/4,715 (0.74%) for step 8, and 351/4,582 (7.66%) for step 11. The non-monotonicity reflects the original discrete lag configurations and available support.

At the 9,371 accepted matches, the Pearson correlation between original MUSE semivariance and GLCM contrast divided by two was r = 0.954 (Spearman 0.962). The median within-patient Pearson correlation among patients with sufficient matches was 0.951, with a patient-resampled 95% interval of approximately [0.948, 0.954]. After removing patient and exact physical-offset-distance effects, the correlation across orientations was 0.846 (cluster-resampled interval [0.828, 0.863]). The median difference between half GLCM contrast and MUSE semivariance was approximately -0.017, with median absolute discrepancy 0.043; these numerical discrepancies reflect nonidentical estimators and are not measurement errors against an assumed ground truth.

4.3 Cohort-level agreement between complete native fields and oriented GLCMs

Cohort-level agreement between complete native fields and oriented GLCMs (︁ 375 2 )︁ = 70,125 possible between-patient combinations, 31,599 (45.1%) satisfied the predetermined volumetric, GLCM, and native-point coverage gates.

Among

The rank correlation between the matched-original-MUSE -field RMSE and the full 468-feature GLCM distance was ρ S = 0.862 (Fig. 5). The association between representations remained stable when examined across repeated patient-subset selections. Under repeated selection of 80% of patients, the descriptive correlation generally ranged from about 0.849 to 0.874; leave-onepatient-out values were approximately 0.859-0.865. Pairwise observations share patients, and these intervals describe patient-subset stability and have no interpretation as independent-pair confidence intervals.

The relationship differed across the six GLCM feature families. Contrasts based on intensity differences were most concordant with the native variographic dissimilarity (GLCM contrast: ρ S = 0.925; dissimilarity: 0.921). GLCM correlation and homogeneity yielded 0.831 and 0.804, whereas entropy and energy yielded 0.652 and 0.606 (Table 2). These results are consistent with a shared second-moment dependence and with the distinct distributional properties measured by co-occurrence entropy and energy. They do not demonstrate that the less concordant feature families contain independently useful clinical information. 2.

4.4 Sampling support explains important apparent discordances

The original diagnostic selection found 15 MUSE -close/GLCM-far pairs under a 20th/80th-percentile comparison, three of which were exported as case examples. Three additional both-close and three both-far pairs were exported as controls; therefore, the nine selected examples are not nine discordances. The three original MUSE -close/GLCM-far examples were UCSF-PDGM-0126/0291, 0340/0430, and 0095/0430. Between 62.9% and 73.0% of their squared robustly scaled GLCM distance came from entropy and energy, and between 61.7% and 72.0% from the largest voxel step. These percentages describe the selected numerical distance, not explained biological variance. The comparison 0340/0430 illustrates the problem: at one step-11 offset, 989 versus 121 contributing voxel pairs yielded entropy 8.529 versus 6.718 bits and energy 0.00344 versus 0.01185. The sparse distribution is especially sensitive to the finite number of distinct observed transitions; because the implementation symmetrized counts, the entropy upper bound for 121 original pairs is log 2 (242) ≈ 7.92 bits, not log 2 (121). Symmetric counting does not render the two sets of spatial pairs statistically independent.

When the previously extracted GLCM feature entries were masked unless both tumors met progressively stricter support gates, representation concordance rose from 0.862 at 100 pairs to 0.871, 0.875, 0.879, and 0.879 at the 200-, 300-, 500-, and 1,000-pair thresholds (Table 3). None of the three originally exported discordant pairs remained in the MUSE -close/GLCM-far category at a gate of 200 or more pairs. Nevertheless, five other discordant pairs remained at the 500-pair threshold, so the evidence concerns the instability of the original examples and does not establish universal equivalence. Excluding entropy and energy increased MUSE -GLCM concordance to 0.913; using only contrast and dissimilarity yielded 0.930. Both operations change the statistical construct and are reported as diagnostic sensitivity checks.

4.5 Molecular association localized to original 3D direction-lag measurements

The primary patient-level association analysis evaluated 2,508 supported original direction-lag cells within 364 grade-4 tumors. The global permutation test after conventional clinical and morphologic adjustment was significant (p = 0.00050). In this model, 894 cells retained an FWER-adjusted p < 0.05 after maxT correction over the tested spatial family. Most were at native physical lags approximately 1 mm to 5 mm; the signed IDH-mutant-minus-wildtype test statistics were negative, compatible with lower conditional semivariance for IDH-mutant tumors in those locations (Table 4). These test scores are standardized residual statistics and should not be interpreted as direct effect sizes.

After the primary addition of five PCs of the full set of 468 oriented GLCM measurements, the global patient-permutation p increased to 0.256, and no cell survived the maxT adjustment. Under the sensitivity specification with ten oriented-GLCM components, the global p was 0.072, with one coordinate nominally passing within-model maxT correction (p FWER = 0.0395): plane 13, local direction 1, original lag 17.4744 mm. The same coordinate had p FWER = 0.1064 in the five-PC primary model. Consequently, that cell was not treated as a robust independent clinical finding. The three tests and their clinical interpretation are summarized in Table 4. The permutation procedure preserved whole-patient labels across the correlated direction-lag cells; 7 of 18 age-volume-sex strata contained both IDH groups, limiting the available conditional exchanges.

A prior secondary analysis of compact fitted MUSE summaries had found conditional associations of local spatial coherence with IDH and overall survival under conventional adjustment. In the 375-patient GLCMmatched cohort, nested five-fold IDH classification yielded ROC-AUC 0.880 using conventional variables, 0.915 after adding that short-range summary, and 0.937 using conventional plus multiscale radiomics/GLCM features. Adding the same MUSE summary to the radiomic model yielded 0.933; including three compact MUSE fitted summaries yielded 0.938. Under regularized Cox modeling, held-out C-index was 0.7010 for conventional plus radiomics/GLCM and 0.7011 after adding the short-range summary. These secondary summary-level comparisons do not test the full native 3D field and are not used as evidence that a compact MUSE representation exhausts its spatial interpretability.

4.6 Full-field examples of spatial-scale and direction-dependent behavior

Original experimental curves of the 29 tumors in the predefined case-study set were inspected together with their 468 oriented GLCM values. Three of the 15 candidate opposite-IDH pairs fell below the 20th percentile of GLCM distance, and none of those three were farther than the 80th percentile of global MUSE field distance. Within this selected set, the two distances were strongly associated (ρ S = 0.907). The examples therefore illustrate how original curves can be read and compared; they do not constitute a selective demonstration of extra molecular discrimination.

The pair UCSF-PDGM-0089 (IDH-mutant) and UCSF-PDGM-0487 (IDH-wildtype) had a nearly identical ET volume (volume ratio 1.012). Its GLCM distance was 0.693, at the 2.8th percentile of the eligible paired-cohort distribution, while the native MUSE comparison RMSE was 0.076, at the 13.7th percentile. Original matched directions nevertheless showed a scale-dependent relative ordering: the mean semivariance difference (mutant minus wildtype) was about -0.070 at 1-3 mm, +0.075 at 6-10 mm, and +0.097 at 10-15 mm. These values are descriptive summaries of matching original curve samples, not new derived patient biomarkers. The GLCM also changes at corresponding offsets; the particular benefit shown here is that the native continuous-lag curves make the change of sign and its physical scale directly legible.

The second pair, UCSF-PDGM-0531 (IDH-mutant) versus UCSF-PDGM-0096 (IDH-wildtype), showed semivariance differences of approximately -0.118 at 3-6 mm, -0.090 at 6-10 mm, -0.018 at 15-20 mm, and +0.128 at 20-30 mm. The latter range was supported by 300 directly paired native directions. In this specific GLCM protocol, the maximum sampled offset is 19.05 mm; the original MUSE curves therefore expose longer-range behavior not represented among the extracted GLCM offset measurements (Fig. 6). This statement concerns the chosen extraction schedule and is not a fundamental inability of GLCMs to sample larger spatial offsets.

At the exact lag of 17.474 mm, the 224 original directions available per tumor show visually different directional semivariance patterns. For 0531, the 10th-90th percentiles across those native directions were approximately 0.764-1.024, versus 0.820-1.008 for 0096. Figure 6 displays the original 3D axial direction vectors at the exact lag, with semivariance represented by point color. The underlying measurements are preserved without angular interpolation. The display is a practical example of the access that a native variographic field provides to the joint geometry of distance and direction. It does not imply a unique molecular signature, because every illustrative pair was constructed to have opposite IDH status and no robust cohort-level incremental association remained after primary GLCM adjustment.

4.7 Independent convolutional classification and post-prediction spatial characterization

The standalone 3D residual CNN was evaluated on 416 independent patients, including 43 IDH-mutant tumors. Pooling the test predictions from the five patient-disjoint outer folds yielded a receiver operating characteristic area under the curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.792 and a precision-recall area under the curve (PR-AUC) of 0.414. The proportion of mutant cases in this population was 10.3%. With a fixed sigmoid decision threshold of 0.5, balanced accuracy was 0.742, sensitivity 0.744, and specificity 0.740. Across the five test folds, individual ROC-AUC values ranged from 0.712 to 0.914; the spread is relevant to the limited number of mutant patients in each fold (8 or 9). The model used MRI intensities and an ET mask only; its results therefore constitute an independent imaging classification benchmark (Table 5).

The independent model can also be inspected on the tumors used for native spatial comparisons. The outof-fold mutant-class scores for UCSF-PDGM-0531 and UCSF-PDGM-0096 were 0.881 and 0.030, respectively, consistent with their mutant and wildtype labels. Their native MUSE fields show distinct direction-and lag-dependent semivariance patterns, including the longer-range relative change described in Section 4.6. For UCSF-PDGM-0089, an IDH-mutant case, the CNN score was 0.096, giving a wildtype classification at the prespecified threshold; the matched wildtype case UCSF-PDGM-0487 received a score of 0.121. Their original MUSE curves remain directly interpretable across physical lag distances irrespective of whether the CNN label was correct. The spatial patterns are an independent description of the image domain and provide no evidence that a CNN classification error can be corrected from these illustrative cases. This pairing demonstrates the intended post-prediction use: a molecular classification accompanied by a traceable spatial characterization of the corresponding tumor.

5. Discussion

The findings are considered in terms of shared spatial information, direct interpretability of the original directional field, reliability of sparse-offset texture estimates, and the limits of molecular association. The implications for further biomedical validation follow from these observed results.

5.1 A framework of shared spatial information with different interpretive representations

This study demonstrates that 3D experimental variograms and multiscale oriented GLCMs capture overlapping aspects of MRI spatial organization. This finding is supported by two measurements based on separate comparison procedures. First, local semivariance and half contrast correlate strongly at physically comparable offsets. Second, original-field MUSE tumor dissimilarities and GLCM tumor dissimilarities show strong rank concordance across more than thirty thousand eligible between-patient comparisons. This agreement is an expected property of two statistical descriptions derived from intensity differences in a common ET domain.

It should not be presented as a methodological limitation or as evidence that the native field can be discarded. Each representation preserves different observational coordinates and statistical properties. MUSE retains a physical separation variable, an axial direction vector, the original semivariance estimate, and the support for every lag, allowing the investigator to inspect the shape and direction-dependent evolution of measured spatial dependence. The six GLCM measures summarize the joint distribution of quantized intensity pairs at a prescribed offset; contrast is closely related to the second moment, whereas entropy and energy depend on how co-occurrence mass is distributed. The wide range of measured intertumor concordance across GLCM families is consistent with this distinction. It does not license the assertion that a residual correlation gap is an estimate of independent biological information.

5.2 What the complete native field adds to biomedical interpretation

The plane-specific illustration in Figs. 3 and 4 clarifies the relationship between measurements and geometric summaries. Four experimental curves supply directly observed scale dependence within one plane, whereas fitted planar and 3D ellipses summarize preferred continuity extents in a compact geometric form. The ellipsoid is therefore an accessible companion visualization of anisotropy, and its fitted semi-axes should not be interpreted as independent measurements or as a substitute for the native 328-curve field. Because the supplied illustration lacks a verified UCSF case identifier, it provides methodological context and carries no patient-level molecular interpretation.

The original field supports questions that can be phrased and visualized in physical terms: at which separation do two tumor regions cease to resemble one another; do certain 3D orientations show greater semivariance than orthogonal orientations; and does the relative pattern change with scale? The original semivariance curves from the selected case pairs illustrate how these properties can be inspected in physical coordinates. In case 0089/0487, the ordering of the semivariance differences changes between short and intermediate separations even though both global GLCM and MUSE dissimilarities are low. In case 0531/0096, the relative ordering changes at long separations, some beyond the largest GLCM offset specified by the analysis protocol. At 17.474 mm, native 3D direction vectors expose different angular structures alongside the full experimental semivariance curves.

These observations document interpretive access, defined here as direct traceability from a spatial claim to its measurement coordinates and physical support. Clinical decision performance, pathological correspondence, and robustness to alternative GLCM offset schedules require additional investigation. In particular, directionresolved GLCM measurements contain explicit orientations as well, and the conclusions depend on the specific set of 78 offsets extracted. A rigorous downstream reader should consider spatial patterns together with the original images and segmentation to evaluate possible effects of mask morphology and heterogeneous enhancement. The original T1c and ET volumes for patient UCSF-PDGM-0531 are shown in Figure 1; direct spatial registration of each variogram direction with the corresponding intratumoral structures remains a target for future validation.

5.3 Support sensitivity is essential to claims of complementarity

Finite voxel-pair support is not merely a technical nuisance. Shape and volume determine which separations occur within an enhancing mask and how many distinct voxel pairs can contribute to a texture estimate. At long offsets, a symmetrized GLCM estimated from few pairs can show inflated energy or constrained entropy even in the absence of a stable biological difference. The loss of the original three MUSE -close/GLCM-far exemplars under stricter saved support thresholds illustrates how a visually persuasive numerical discordance can arise from measurement design. Support-aware interpretation is therefore required for comparisons between the representations.

Similarly, only a subset of GLCM offsets could be paired exactly to original MUSE lags and angular directions within the selected geometric tolerances. A nonmatchable offset does not establish information missing from GLCM. MUSE and GLCM also aggregate voxel pairs differently. Comparisons at equal distance or approximate orientation are useful diagnostics, but equating their estimates without matching intensity representation and pair sets would be incorrect. These considerations are relevant to any prospective standard for joint radiomic-geostatistical analysis.

5.4 Independent prediction and descriptive interpretation

The CNN establishes a patient-level image-classification reference that can accompany the directional spatial profile measured by MUSE . Its voxel-based residual layers learn predictive representations from the T1c crop and ET geometry, while the archived MUSE field exposes a physical lag axis, 3D orientations, semivariance, and voxel-pair support. Reporting the two outputs for the same case makes both a molecular prediction and a directly inspectable account of spatial signal organization available for biomedical assessment. Such post-prediction characterization has a different evidential meaning from attribution techniques that estimate which voxels or learned representations caused a network output. No internal CNN attribution map, causal explanation, or clinical decision improvement is established by the present experiment.

The standalone CNN obtained a cohort-level ROC-AUC of 0.792, with substantial variation across the five external test folds of the internal validation protocol. The 375-case subset ROC-AUC of 0.808 is a descriptive restriction of predictions produced by models trained on the larger 416-case cohort. The CNN and radiomic classification experiments differed in preprocessing, feature domain, fitting protocol, and, in some cases, evaluation population. Their AUC values are therefore not a paired head-to-head estimate of model superiority. The complete MUSE field was evaluated principally through physical interpretation and support-aware comparison with GLCMs, not as a predictive replacement for the CNN. A prospective clinical evaluation could test whether access to the spatial description changes readers' assessment, error detection, or downstream decisions beyond the image and classifier score alone.

5.5 Clinical associations and the distinction between association and utility

The native-field grade-4 IDH analysis detected a broad association with semivariance under conventional adjustment. The predominantly short-range and negative signed cell scores are compatible with a shift in short-range spatial coherence, a biologically interpretable aspect of intratumoral MRI organization. The strong attenuation after adjusting for directional GLCM components is equally informative. No native direction-lag cell remained significant under the primary five-PC oriented-GLCM model, and the global IDH test was not significant. A single significant cell under the ten-PC sensitivity model depended on the adjustment specification. The study therefore does not substantiate an independent MUSE -derived IDH marker beyond the radiomic representation tested.

Prior summary-level analyses also associated a MUSE short-range measure with IDH and overall survival after conventional adjustment. The same measure was strongly reconstructable from harmonized multiscale radiomics in held-out folds and did not improve internally validated IDH AUC or survival C-index once the enriched radiomic model was available. These secondary analyses characterize the overlap of specific scalar summaries; they neither replace the 328 original MUSE curves nor define the clinical performance of the complete field. Molecular subtype is a clinically relevant annotation, but it is not synonymous with the directly observable geometric structure of enhancement, and the 2021 WHO framework integrates multiple molecular and histologic variables [1].

The opposite-IDH case pairs provide illustrative comparisons; molecular association is evaluated at the patient-cohort level. All selected tumors were grade 4; they cannot be interpreted as examples of differing WHO severity grade. Survival comparisons require censoring-aware adjusted analyses across patients. A demonstrable clinical benefit would require an independently validated increase in prognosis, prediction, stratification, or clinical decision quality, ideally in multicenter data and with explicit controls for treatment and selection. The present results instead justify a more precise claim of descriptive and physical interpretability with a measured, and partly shared, relationship to molecular annotation.

6. Conclusions, Limitations and Future Work

We introduced and evaluated an integrated radiomic-geostatistical workflow that preserves the original 328-curve 3D MUSE experimental representation per enhancing glioma and places it in explicit correspondence with multiscale directional GLCM statistics. The paired experiments demonstrate strong shared second-order spatial information, family-dependent differences in co-occurrence texture, and substantial sensitivity of apparent discordance to spatial pair support. Original patient examples show the value of retaining scale and 3D orientation as directly inspectable physical coordinates, including behavior beyond the specific GLCM offsets extracted. In the WHO grade-4 subgroup, a widespread MUSE -IDH association under conventional adjustment was largely accounted for by oriented GLCM components, and no robust incremental clinical association was established in the primary adjusted analysis. An independent 3D CNN provided out-of-fold IDH classification (ROC-AUC 0.792 over 416 patients), and its predictions were inspected alongside the native spatial measurements in selected tumors. This joint reporting provides a classification outcome accompanied by physically interpretable tumor heterogeneity measurements, while clinical benefit from such post-prediction characterization remains to be established. The framework thus offers a traceable basis for complementary image classification and spatial tumor description.

Limitations and future work. Several limitations define the external validity of the study. The source is one public imaging collection, with no independent institutional validation and no test-retest acquisition. The ET-restricted analysis excludes 75 examinations without enhancement and consequently represents predominantly grade-4 tumors; the native-field IDH analysis includes only 24 mutant cases, limiting power and exchangeability within the stratified permutations. Small or elongated tumor regions provide few voxel pairs at large offsets, which affects both MUSE and GLCM estimates. Normal-score and GLCM discretization choices, directional bandwidth, angle tolerances, and selected offset lengths affect the quantitative comparison. The original MUSE field is fully available, but the global between-tumor RMSE weights lag-direction observations according to coverage and cannot summarize every scale and orientation uniformly. The preselected clinical examples were examined post hoc, and the full spatial association test was developed in the course of the study; it is exploratory with no independent preregistered confirmation.

Adjustment by five or ten principal components of oriented GLCM features is deliberately finite-dimensional and cannot guarantee conditional independence from all 468 original oriented GLCM measurements, let alone arbitrary nonlinear transformations. The native 3D direction-lag coordinates are strongly dependent, and the number of candidate cells is not the number of independent biological signals. Permutations within age-volume-sex strata rely on conditional exchangeability; only seven strata contained both IDH classes. Tumor mask perturbation, repeated-image assessment, and scanner-to-scanner reproducibility remain untested in this analysis. A representative anatomical and ET overlay was generated from the original NIfTI image for UCSF-PDGM-0531; corresponding anatomy-to-direction inspection was not systematically performed for every selected case. The CNN was trained from scratch on a small and imbalanced single-cohort dataset with 8-9 mutant cases per outer test fold; we report internal out-of-fold discrimination without independent-center evaluation or score calibration. The ET mask was supplied as a CNN input, so its classification cannot be attributed to intensity patterns alone. The classifier and radiomic models were not matched in every training and preprocessing detail; comparing their reported AUC values does not establish predictive superiority. Finally, there is no prospective or external demonstration that the combined reporting of CNN predictions and MUSE spatial fields improves clinical decisions or patient outcomes. These limitations motivate three connected next steps: evaluate stability of the original directional field under controlled mask perturbations and acquisition variation; extend the analysis to multiple tumor compartments and modalities where comparable spatial support exists; and test predefined, interpretable native-field hypotheses in independently annotated clinical cohorts. Future work can assess CNN and MUSE observations together in blinded clinical reader studies, preserving the original physical variogram observations and evaluating their effect on interpretation separately from the predictive accuracy of the network. These extensions require new evidence and define subsequent validation objectives.

Data availability.

The publicly accessible UCSF-PDGM imaging collection, segmentation masks, and associated annotations are available through The Cancer Imaging Archive (dataset DOI: https://doi.org/10.7937/tcia.bdgf-8v37). All data produced in this work are retained in the authors' research archives and have not yet been deposited in a public repository.

Code availability.

The open-source MUSE methodological implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/mariannamiola/muse. The MRI-specific scripts for data preparation, directional GLCM extraction, statistical evaluation, and standalone CNN benchmarking have not yet been deposited in that repository. The publicly accessible MUSE code and the study-specific analysis scripts are distinct components of the workflow.

Competing interests and funding. The authors declare no competing interests nor funding.

Use of generative artificial intelligence.

During the preparation of this work, the authors used ChatGPT 5.6 (OpenAI) to assist with the revision of the work.

Ethics and consent. This is a retrospective computational analysis of an openly shared, de-identified public imaging dataset. Dataset governance and conditions of reuse follow the UCSF-PDGM/TCIA release [18]. No new human participant recruitment or intervention was performed.

A.1 Native MUSE archive and GLCM settings

The source run used automatic plane generation and four sampled directions on each of 82 planes (328 3D curves per patient). The original export retained h, ˆ︁ γ, N and source SHA256 for each point. The 375-case GLCM file was generated with steps {1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 11}, 13 3D axial offsets, normal-score rank mapping and a quantization bin width of 0.2. Six measures were available for every supported offset: contrast, dissimilarity, homogeneity, correlation, entropy, and energy. Invariant summaries were calculated only within physically equivalent offset-length classes. Source parameters were frozen before the comparison studies.

A.2 Secondary radiomic overlap experiments

Earlier internally cross-validated models examined how much of selected MUSE -derived summaries could be statistically reconstructed from standard and multiscale radiomic features. The original omnidirectional curve comparison showed a held-out R² of approximately 0.776 for its specified regression experiment; the published-in-project direction-dispersion target had R² = 0.546 with conventional multiscale radiomics and up to 0.689 after additional directional GLCM information. These results assess predictability of auxiliary MUSE targets; they do not reconstruct or replace original MUSE measurements in the present study, and 1 -R² is a regression diagnostic and has no direct interpretation as a proportion of exclusive biological information.

For completeness, the clinical benchmark based on one short-range MUSE summary obtained held-out ROC-AUC 0.880 using conventional clinical/morphologic variables and 0.915 after adding that summary. The radiomics/GLCM-enriched model achieved 0.937, versus 0.933 with the summary added and 0.938 after adding three compact fitted summaries. A ridge-Cox internal analysis of the paired cohort yielded C-index 0.7010 for clinical/radiomic variables and 0.7011 after adding the summary. This appendix records the secondary comparison and explicitly separates it from inference based on the original full directional field.

A.3 Interpretation safeguards

The original MUSE archive, the GLCM table, and the clinical labels are different observational layers. The first describes spatial dependence conditional on an ET mask, the second a quantized transition distribution, and the third molecular, clinical, or time-to-event annotations. An association between any two layers is not evidence of anatomical causality, histological validation, or downstream clinical utility. The patient-level nature of the sampling unit, the influence of finite spatial support, and the reported null results after radiomic adjustment apply equally to all illustrated examples.
